# PREFACE.

The HUMAN MIND is born with the desire to know Nature; whoever wishes to succeed in this must study its causes through their effects. <note>The author is describing the empirical method—the scientific approach of observing physical results to understand the hidden laws of nature.</note>

Can he perceive its mechanism and the knowledge of the rules it follows too soon, so as not to delve deeper into the means of studying its principles? On the contrary, he surrenders himself to all manner of errors— <note>The text here breaks off, suggesting that without a proper understanding of natural laws, the human mind is prone to falling into scientific or philosophical fallacies.</note>

A 3

<summary>This preface introduces the innate human curiosity regarding the natural world and argues that true knowledge is gained by studying the relationship between cause and effect.</summary>

The operations of Nature
are simple, and the systems of

in proportion to his care;
can he ever study too deeply the principles

# THE DIADEM
# OF THE SAGES,

->OR<-

# DEMONSTRATION
### OF
## INFERIOR NATURE.

---

# PRINCIPES
### *And Causes of Nature.*

By Nature, one understands the entire World, composed of the heavens and the earth, and of everything that depends upon them; this is the object of true Philosophy <note>In the historical context, "Philosophy" refers to Natural Philosophy—the study of the physical universe and its laws before the modern separation of sciences.</note>, and what a student of Nature <term>Physicien|In the 17th and 18th centuries, a "Physicien" was a natural philosopher or physicist who studied the properties of matter, rather than a medical doctor.</term> must know; and since to know is to understand things by their cause and their principles, it

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the formal treatise, defining "Nature" as the totality of the heavens and earth and establishing it as the primary subject for the natural philosopher.</summary>

<keywords>Nature, Natural Philosophy, Physicien, Inferior Nature, Principles of Nature, cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 17, defining the duties of the natural philosopher and delving into Aristotelian causality, which was the standard framework for understanding the physical world in the early modern period.</meta>

# THE DIADEM

is certain that the entire care of a Natural Phi-
losopher <note>Continuing from the previous page, "Physicien" refers to one who studies natural laws.</note> must be to seek the causes
& the principles of Nature, and of every-
thing that happens in the world.

I do not examine here if there is some
difference between cause & principle,
for I believe that everything which is a principle
is a cause, in its own way, of that of which it
is the principle; no one doubts that
everything which is a cause is a principle.

The relationship & the connection between the cause
& the effect do not permit one to
give a true notion of the cause
without including the effect within it.

The most noble of causes, according to the
Philosophers, is called *efficient*,
since, strictly speaking, it
alone has an effect, although this
occurs in different ways.

If this cause acts by a virtue <note>original: "vertue" — in alchemy and natural philosophy, a "virtue" is an inherent power or specific quality that allows a substance to produce an effect.</note> which
is its own, it is called the *principal*
*cause*; if it operates only by the virtue
& by the impression of another, it is
an *instrumental cause*.

<meta>Continuing from page 19, which introduced the existence of a first principle as a self-evident truth, this page provides the foundational argument for that principle by using the reality of human existence as its starting point.</meta>

# THE DIADEM

For this purpose, I assume a truth known to everyone, which no one can dispute without being considered ridiculous or insane.

This truth, which will serve as my principle for proving the existence of a **first cause**, is drawn from our own existence: there is nothing so evident and so certain as this fact; namely, that we are in the world. The testimony of our senses attests to this truth; what we think, what we say, and what we do does not allow us to believe that our existence is an illusion. It is therefore certain, and more than evident, that we are in the world: now, we cannot be here by ourselves, and through ourselves, nor by chance and fortuitously, nor by **necessity of being** <note>In metaphysics, "necessity of being" refers to something that exists because it is impossible for it not to exist; the author argues that humans are "contingent" rather than "necessary."</note>; we must therefore be here by the means and by the benefit of someone else who was the author and the **free principle** <note>A "free principle" (principe libre) refers to a causal agent that acts by its own will and choice, rather than being driven by blind mechanical forces.</note> of the being that we possess.

<meta>catchword: There [Voilà]</meta>

# OF THE SAGES.

Thus the **first cause** <note>In philosophy, the "First Cause" or *causa prima* is the initial cause of all things, often identified as God in this context.</note>, which some have sought to deny, is well-established upon the truth of our being.

Aristotle <note>original: "Ariftote"</note>, who teaches the eternity of the world and the immortality of souls, admits an **actual infinite** <note>An "actual infinite" refers to a completed set of infinite things. Aristotle famously argued against the existence of an actual infinite in the physical world, though the author here suggests his system implies it.</note> in Nature: if the world is eternal, it has had no beginning; this first cause is then useless; here lies a contradiction, since the same reason that proves the beginning of the world proves the existence of a first cause; and the same reason that proves this existence shows that the world began to be, and that it is not eternal.

The opposition and the relationship that necessarily exists between the **principle** <note>The "principle" here refers to the active agent or creator.</note> that acts and the **subject** upon which it acts introduce a necessary distinction.

# REMARKS

### On the *Acidum Pingue* of Mr. MEYER. <term>Acidum Pingue: Latin for "fat acid." Proposed by Johann Friedrich Meyer in 1764, this was a hypothetical "fire-matter" or universal acid used to explain the chemical changes in substances like lime when heated. It was a major topic of debate in 18th-century chemistry before the modern understanding of gases.</term>

<margin>(a) Genesis, first chapter, verse 6.</margin>
By closely considering the separation of the lower waters from the upper waters $(a)$, we have reason to believe that these waters were nothing but an aqueous vapor, or a dark humidity; for if among all created substances, moisture alone is a subject capable of receiving every form, it can be, consequently, the subject upon which the entire work of creation turned. <note>In this context, "subject" refers to the underlying material or "prime matter" that lacks a specific shape until acted upon by a creative force.</note>

This dark chaos being formless and a confused mass suitable for all forms, and indifferent to all, necessarily had to have the essence of a moist vapor.

It is always noted that in

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural philosophy and creation, moving from the general concept of "chaos" mentioned on page 45 to the specific alchemical process of "re-incrudation"—how matter returns to its basic state to allow for new growth.</meta>

->THE DIADEM<-

<note>Translator's note: Text on the right-hand margin is cut off in the source due to cropping or tight binding; missing letters have been reconstructed for readability.</note>

...all the productions of the internal world, the seeds are clothed in an aqueous humor, and that vegetable seeds, when cast into the earth to be returned to their raw state <term>re-incrudated: an alchemical term for returning a substance to its "crude" or original state so it can undergo transformation</term>, reduce themselves into a mucilaginous humidity; and no generation occurs in any kingdom whatsoever—be it animal, vegetable, or mineral—until first the seeds are reduced to their first matter, a true chaos, which the Philosopher <note>In historical texts, "The Philosopher" usually refers to a practitioner of alchemy or natural science.</note> must perfectly know.

One sees by this that this aqueous matter, or dark vapor, was undoubtedly the prime matter of this formless mass, and of this embryo of the world, which was to serve as the base and foundation for all generations.

The prime matter is twofold; that is to say, sulfur and mercury (according to the common folk), which is a humidity of the air mixed with heat; and this humidity has been named by the Philosophers, *radical moisture or mercury of the body.* <note>The "radical moisture" was believed to be the essential fluid within all matter that preserved life and form.</note>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical principle that all life and matter must first return to a state of "chaos" or "prime matter"—a moist, vaporous state—before they can be reborn or transformed. It identifies this original substance as a combination of sulfur and mercury, referred to by scholars as the "radical moisture."</summary>

<keywords>prime matter, chaos, re-incrudation, radical moisture, sulfur, mercury, alchemical generation, natural philosophy</keywords>

->O F  T H E  S A G E S.<-

This is the true *acidum pingue* <term>acidum pingue: Latin for "fatty acid." In 18th-century chemistry, this was a hypothetical substance thought to explain the properties of caustic lime.</term> <note>original: "acidum pingue"</note> that Meyer <note>Johann Friedrich Meyer (1705–1765), a German chemist who proposed that limestone became caustic lime by absorbing a "fatty acid" from fire.</note> believed he had demonstrated as a caustic substance that could be found, according to him, in lime or other limestone rocks—that is to say, a caustic substance *par excellence*. I maintain the opposite: that it can never be found in a dead thing. The *acidum pingue* is not caustic at all; on the contrary, it is benign. It is the soul of Nature, the fire joined to moisture, which is the principle of life for all compounds. It is the informing form <note>In traditional philosophy, the "informing form" is the internal principle that gives a substance its specific nature and life.</note> joined with matter, the agent and the patient of Nature, the active and the passive. Finally, it is the prime matter of all created bodies—consequently, a spirit of life that can only be found in living and animated bodies. It would, on the contrary, be destructive if it were caustic, because every caustic substance carries with it the destruction of all compounds. However little a Physicist <note>Historically, a "physicist" referred to anyone studying natural philosophy and the laws of the physical world.</note> might wish to study Nature, with even a little

<summary>The author critiques the chemist Johann Friedrich Meyer, arguing that the "acidum pingue" (fatty acid) is not a corrosive, dead chemical found in lime, but rather a vital, life-giving spirit found only in living things. This substance is described as the "soul of Nature" and the essential "prime matter" that balances active fire and passive moisture.</summary>

<keywords>acidum pingue, Johann Friedrich Meyer, lime, caustic substances, prime matter, soul of Nature, informing form, principle of life, alchemical physics</keywords>

### LE DIADÊME

We wish to dissolve, yet we do nothing but separate the parts; Nature does the opposite; she has but two objectives: to dissolve and to coagulate, to separate the pure from the impure; she assimilates only with homogeneous <note>Sharing the same nature or substance.</note> parts and rejects those that are heterogeneous to her; whereas Art mixes both, without distinction or understanding of the cause. # DISSERTATION
## *Interesting regarding the Universal Medicine.*

I do not doubt that the **Hermetic Philosophers** <term>Hermetic Philosophers: Followers of the legendary Hermes Trismegistus; a traditional term for practitioners of alchemy who sought to transform matter and spirit.</term>, who possess the **spagyric secret** <term>spagyric secret: The art of separating and then recombining the components of natural substances (usually plants or minerals) to produce potent medicines.</term>, will rise up against me for speaking with too much clarity; indeed, they would be right, if the honor of God and the benefit of one’s neighbor did not have more authority than their private interests. It is only to the **Children of Doctrine** <note>original: "Enfants de Doctrine." This refers to initiated students or "sons of art" who have proven themselves worthy of learning alchemical secrets.</note> that I address these words, to whom I today open the secrets of **occult philosophy**, to make visible to the eye and tangible to the touch the true interpretation of the writings of the Sages. These writings were used by the inhabitants of the **chemical mountain** <note>The "chemical mountain" is a frequent allegorical symbol in alchemical texts, representing the difficult path of labor and purification required to reach the Philosopher’s Stone.</note> to hide their **Earth** <note>In alchemy, "Earth" often refers to the "Philosophical Earth" or the primary material (Prima Materia) that serves as the foundation of the Great Work.</note> from the impious sworn enemies of God and the learned foster-children of Nature; and by this

<summary>The author begins a dissertation on the Universal Medicine, justifying his decision to reveal alchemical secrets for the glory of God and the help of others despite the traditional secrecy of the Hermetic Sages.</summary>

## CONCERNING THE WISE ##

...undoubtedly sought the means to cure his illnesses in order to preserve for a longer time a life that is always ready to escape him.

It did not require much meditation for him to conceive and convince himself that the **principle** <note>original: "principe" — In 18th-century natural philosophy, this refers to the fundamental constituent or "vital spark" of a substance.</note> which constitutes his body, and which sustains it, was also that which ought to preserve it in its state of being: the natural appetite for **nourishment** <note>original: "alimens" — Referring generally to food or any substance that sustains the body's "fire."</note> indicated this to him clearly enough. But he soon perceived that these foods, as perishable as himself, carried within his interior a **principle of death** along with the **principle of life**.

It was therefore necessary to reason upon the beings of the **Universe**, and to meditate for a long time to discover this **fruit of life**, capable of leading Man almost to immortality.

It was not enough to have perceived this **treasure** <note>A recurring alchemical metaphor for the "Philosopher's Stone" or the "Universal Medicine" hidden within nature.</note> through the **envelope** <note>original: "l'enveloppe" — The physical or "gross" outer shell of matter that masks its inner, spiritual essence.</note> that covers and hides it from the eyes of the common man, in order to make use of this fruit

D 2

The great secrecy observed by the Priests, and the high Sciences they professed, caused them to be respected and esteemed throughout Egypt; both during the long years when they had no communication with foreigners, and after they granted them freedom of trade, Egypt was always regarded as the Seminary <note>In this context, a "seedbed" or foundational source where knowledge is cultivated.</note> of Sciences and Arts; Philo the Jew <note>original: "Philon Juif"; also known as Philo of Alexandria (c. 20 BCE – 50 CE), a Hellenistic Jewish philosopher who sought to harmonize the Torah with Greek philosophy.</note>, in the first Book of the *Life of Moses*, reports that

St. Clement of Alexandria <note>A 2nd-century Christian theologian who believed that much of Greek and Egyptian wisdom was derived from older Hebrew sources.</note> expresses himself in the same terms as Philo; but he adds Medicine and the knowledge of hieroglyphs, which the Priests taught only to the children of the Kings of the country, and to their own.

->### OF THE SAGES.<-

->D 3<-

effect; the profound knowledge they had fundamentally acquired regarding **Nature** suggests that they possessed too much enlightenment to have fallen into such gross absurdity—an absurdity so far removed from the common sense and reason granted to all men since the creation of the world.

Even the ingenious **fables** they invented to characterize the **divine virtues** within Nature and the secret art of its operations are fictions under which they concealed her mysteries. They viewed these mysteries as having their source in the wisdom of the **Prime Mover** <note>A philosophical term, often associated with Aristotle, referring to the supreme being or original cause of all motion and existence in the universe.</note>, whose respectable majesty required such discretion regarding a **coarse and profane people** <note>The "uninitiated" or general public, whom the author believes would misunderstand or disrespect sacred truths.</note>, who tend to turn the most sacred things into contempt and evil; thus, this concealment was the result of their **prudence**.

I will conclude this article by demonstrating the origin and administration of **Medicine** through the personage of the **Canons of Notre-Dame de Paris** <note>The high-ranking clergy attached to the Cathedral of Notre Dame, who historically oversaw charitable and medical functions in the city.</note>, or Officers—

<page-num>87</page-num>

# CONCERNING THE SAGES #

It is only through our reason that we succeed in discovering that which is hidden <note>original: "occulte." In this 18th-century philosophical context, "occult" refers to things that are secret, veiled, or not immediately apparent to the physical senses, rather than specifically "magic."</note>, and our reason itself is just as hidden as everything else.

The storm rises within ourselves; a favorable wind seems to carry us away from the reef, even as a contrary wind pushes us toward it. Let us not fear running aground, provided that we listen to the voice of our intelligence.

If the sound of the waves or the roar of the storm pushes aside this salutary <note>Meaning health-giving or beneficial.</note> voice for a few moments, let us not cease to lend an attentive ear; it will soon break through with brilliance, and lead us to the harbor of tranquility and happiness.

<header># ON THE WISE #</header>

...and although he has the will, he does not always have the strength to resist. Has he received an insult? Or if some accident happens to him, is he free to forget it totally? He is forced despite himself to remember it, even in the moment when he would wish to have no idea of it.

I view the will <note>original: "volonté"</note> as a faculty of the soul, insofar as it understands, as it judges, and as it wills; so that this faculty exists only in potentiality <note>original: "en puissance." This refers to the philosophical concept of 'potential' versus 'actual'—the soul has the capacity to will things even when it is not actively doing so.</note> when the soul ceases to operate.

In the midst of the immense chain of beings <note>This refers to the "Great Chain of Being," a popular historical concept that everything in the universe has a specific, hierarchical place, from minerals to God.</note> where all the parts are linked to one another by mutual action and reaction, and where everything follows a necessary path, the soul alone is independent; it alone prescribes laws to itself. Thus, it is from within himself that Man draws the idea of liberty. If he only contemplated the material order, he could never form the simplest notion of it.

It is not granted to everyone to penetrate to the sanctuary of the secrets of Nature: very few people know the path that leads there. Some, being impatient, go astray by taking paths that seem to shorten the journey; others find, at almost every step, crossroads that confuse them, turn to the left, and go to Tartarus <note>In Greek mythology, Tartarus is the deep abyss used as a place of punishment for the wicked.</note>, instead of keeping to the right which leads to the Elysian Fields <note>The final resting place of the souls of the heroic and the virtuous.</note>, because they do not have, like Aeneas <note>The hero of Virgil’s *Aeneid*, who travels through the underworld.</note>, a Sibyl <note>A priestess and prophetess; specifically, the Cumaean Sibyl who acted as Aeneas’s guide in the underworld.</note> for a guide. <page-num>93</page-num>
<header>OF THE WISE MEN.</header>

With Nature as their guide, they worshipped the Supreme Being <note>The "Supreme Being" (L'Être Suprême) was a popular concept in the 18th century, particularly among Deists, referring to a creator deity whose existence is revealed through the natural laws of the universe rather than through specific religious revelation.</note> without knowing Him, by paying homage to that same Nature and to its principal matter in essence; for they held as a maxim and point of doctrine that everything which had life possessed it only as a celestial origin; Ovid himself testified to this sentiment, saying that *God is in us* <note>original: "Dieu est en nous." This likely refers to the Latin phrase *Est deus in nobis*, found in the works of the Roman poet Ovid (e.g., *Fasti* and *Ars Amatoria*), suggesting a spark of divinity resides within humanity.</note>.

Cicero and all the great men of Antiquity spoke and thought likewise; thus they recognized a God as the author of Nature and of all things, infused as He is by His eternal spirit operating within it and acting as their preserver.

Experience also shows that nothing grows, nothing vegetates in the midst of those *fertilizers* <note>original: "engrais"; refers to manures or soil additives believed at the time to contain "salts" of fertility.</note> which have always been believed to contain the true principle of fertility. History mentions various lands made sterile by sowing salt; but this salt, we are told, was perhaps not the one to which fertility is attributed; moreover, due to its excessive quantity, it overheated the soil, "burning" it, so to speak, and thus necessarily produced a bad effect. <page-num>108</page-num>

# THE DIADEM

These portions of water offer in every direction only an infinitely slender and subtle surface, which Nature has so admirably distributed: lining the interior of the fibers, they soon become a part of them by thickening; other particles then follow suit in their turn, and as the plant successively develops, it allows entry to new drops of water which gradually take on a consistency, a scent, a color, and a flavor proportioned to the degree of <term>coction</term> <note>original: "cuisson"; the author uses the metaphor of "cooking" or ripening to describe how heat transforms liquid sap into solid plant tissue.</note> that the different shapes and various aspects under which they are exposed to the heat allow them to receive.

Whatever ideas one may have regarding Vegetation, one is always obliged to turn to water in the development of the germ of all plants; one feels the need they have for it in their growth and production, and everyone knows that from the moment a plant is deprived of moisture, it perishes without remedy.

Having established that water particles thicken into plant fibers, the author now explains why water specifically—due to its neutrality—is the only substance capable of being transformed into the diverse qualities of different plants.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>

<center>OF THE SAGES.</center>

Not just any moisture indiscriminately makes the earth fertile; it can only be that of water. Plants need a food that they can easily change into their own nature; first, this food must be capable of taking on all kinds of shapes, since it must adopt those of the different parts to which it attaches; plants also require a substance that has no taste of its own, so that they can give it their own unique flavor; as each plant has its own particular and unique taste, it is easier for it to bestow it upon a being that has none at all, rather than forcing it to change an existing one. The same applies to scent, color, and any other qualities whatsoever; common sense alone is enough to see that all bodies which already possess any of these qualities in a formed state present, by that very fact, an obstacle and further difficulties to the operations of <term>Vegetation</term> <note>In this 18th-century context, "Vegetation" refers to the active, vital process of growth and the internal chemistry of the plant, rather than just the plants themselves.</note>.

<vocab>moisture, fertile earth, plants, food, taste, scent, color, Vegetation</vocab>

*Water* is perhaps, after mercury, the one among all solid bodies that enters most easily into fusion <note>The author reflects an older scientific view where liquids were considered solids that had been "melted" or fused by heat; water was thus seen as "melted ice."</note>, to the point that in our climates it is rare, even in winter, for the atmosphere not to have sufficient heat to keep it liquefied. The consequence of these truths is found in Nature; she never contradicts herself. Thus, by providing a soil with many salts, one provides it with a greater number of small reservoirs; one furnishes it with more means to preserve particles *of water* and to provide itself with them according to its needs.

Thus, gravel is a terrain incapable of **Vegetation**, for lack of water.

Without traversing every field, it is enough to observe that each soil is more or less fertile in proportion to how closely it approaches the nature of the soils mentioned above; that is to say, in proportion to its ability to retain more or less water. Give to a field of sand, or of gravel, a portion of this soil, thus strong <note>In historical agriculture, "strong soil" (terre forte) usually refers to clay-heavy or dense earth that holds together firmly.</note>, which retains enough water for it, & you will have abundant Vegetation.

The meadows (without considering the nature of their terrain) form a fabric of an infinite number of small roots that intertwine in every direction, and are thereby capable of slowing the runoff of water. Provide water abundantly to your meadows; their surface is in a state to preserve it for themselves; you will have fertility.

Water is the nourishment of plants; it is only natural that its excess should be harmful to them; and to see how this excess of water can delay **Vegetation**, suspend it, and even prevent it entirely, let us bring the torch of Physics <note>In the 18th century, "Physics" (Physique) referred broadly to "Natural Philosophy"—the study of the physical laws of the natural world.</note> into this admirable and mysterious mechanism of Nature.

Night is intended for the nourishment of plants, and day for their digestion: during the night, Nature spreads a *nourishing* dew everywhere, and the heat of the day effects the secretion <note>original: "sécrétion"; in 18th-century biology, this refers to the process of separating solid nutrients from the liquid sap to build the plant's structure.</note> of the nutrients taken during the night; this is how this alternation of coolness and heat, of day and night, of wetting and <term>desiccation</term> <note>original: "desséchement"; the process of thorough drying that allows the "sediment" of the sap to harden into wood or leaf.</note> serves all vegetation; this is why even the human eye, as coarse as it is, believes it sees plants grow during the night, or at least notices their progress after the rain.

One can rather conceive of a constant rest within extension; but as the Author doubtless returns to the primary cause, which is God, it is certain that He is outside of extension, but this is within the intellectual realm <note>The "intellectual realm" here refers to the world of pure spirit or mind, as opposed to the "sensory" world of matter.</note>.

In the sensory realm, there can be no movement without extension: whether it be the effect of the action, or the action itself—though they are inseparable—there must necessarily be extension, either in the action or in the effect. Action is never without movement, or to put it better, action and movement are one and the same thing; from this it follows that a being at rest begins to move when it begins to act.

### FIRST RULE.

> "Every body that is not in motion perseveres in its state of rest; and every body that is in motion continues to move in the same direction and with the same degree of speed it received, until a new cause <note>In modern physics, this 'cause' is defined as a force.</note> obliges it to change its state."

### SECOND RULE.

> "The change that occurs in the movement of a body is always proportional to the cause that produces it, and it always occurs along a straight line."

<term>Universal Medicine: Also known as the "Panacea" or the "Elixir of Life," this refers to the ultimate goal of alchemy—a substance capable of curing all diseases, prolonging life, and perfecting both metals and the human soul.</term>

<note>The claim that this text originates from an "Anglo-Saxon Prince" and has "never been translated nor copied" is a common literary device in 18th-century esoteric manuscripts. By claiming ancient or noble origins, the author grants the text an air of lost, "primitive" authority and exclusivity.</note>

His choice fell upon Mercury, son of Maia <note>In Roman mythology, Mercury (Greek: Hermes) is the messenger of the gods; in alchemy, "Mercury" represents the volatile principle, the spirit that mediates between the fixed (earth) and the celestial (heaven).</note>. This God seemed to him more fit than any other to fulfill this august and important function, because his wings and his lightness provided him with the means to go and return often from Heaven to Earth and from Earth to Heaven, to carry the message of the two lovers who took it upon themselves to compose the <term>Ambrosia</term> <note>The food of the gods; here, it symbolizes the "Elixir of Life" or the "Philosopher's Stone" in liquid form.</note>, with which the newlyweds wished to regale the lower Gods, this dish being destined to give them the immortality which they did not yet enjoy.

By the third arrow, already like a sublime eagle he dares to fix his gaze upon you <note>The "eagle" is a common alchemical symbol for sublimation. Here, "to fix" (French: *fixer*) carries a double meaning: the eagle's legendary ability to look directly at the sun, and the chemical "fixation" of a volatile substance into a solid state.</note>; by the seventh, he is equal to you? It is no longer in your power to take from him the virtue <note>original: "vertu"; in this context, it refers to the inherent power, medicinal efficacy, or "soul" of the substance.</note> that you have given him, and far from desiring it, you only long for the happy moment when, freed from the rest of his impurities, your brother—who is your son <note>The Philosopher's Stone is often called the "Son of the Sun," as it is produced from the essence of gold but perfected by the Art.</note>—will reign with glory, and will crown your other children <note>The "children" refers to the base metals which the finished Philosopher’s Stone has the power to "crown" or transmute into gold.</note>.

Furthermore, from the body of the King, Mercury composed the *elixir of the Sages*; it was the Ambrosia <note>In mythology, the food of the gods that grants immortality; here, it refers to the life-extending properties of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of the banquet of the Gods at the wedding feasts of Heaven and Earth, which concluded immediately.

<page-num>240</page-num>

# THE DIADEM #

Jupiter was pleased with the labors of the son of Maya <note>Maya's son is Mercury (the Greek Hermes), the messenger of the gods and the patron deity of alchemy, from whom the "Hermetic" arts take their name.</note>, and to show him his satisfaction, he allowed Mercury to multiply by ten, and ten times ten, and ten times a hundred, and ten times a thousand, the *Elixir of the Sages*, as much in power as in quantity. This was done simply by bathing the King and the Queen, his wife, in the blood of the Red Servants and the Cold Women <note>This describes the process of "Multiplication," where the potency of the Philosopher's Stone is increased by adding more of the refined base materials—sulfur (Red Servants) and mercury (Cold Women).</note>, which Mercury had kept in reserve within the serpents of his caduceus <note>The caduceus is the staff carried by Mercury; its two entwined serpents represent the reconciliation of opposing forces (like heat and cold, or spirit and matter).</note>. To these servants, the King and Queen distributed, as a reward, kingdoms as great as their own. Since that time, Ambrosia <note>In mythology, the food of the gods that grants immortality; here, it represents the perfected Alchemical Stone.</note> has been the ordinary dish at the table of the Gods, and very rarely do they share it with a few Sages, their favorites, who, fearing the gods, thank them, do good, enjoy their gift, and remain silent <note>A reference to the "Hermetic Silence," the vow taken by alchemists never to reveal the secret of the Stone to the unworthy.</note>.

->THE END.<-

<meta>This page transitions from the Table of Contents to the formal legal front matter of the book. In 18th-century France, every published work required an official endorsement from a royal censor and a formal "Permission" from the King to ensure the content was neither seditious nor heretical.</meta>

# APPROVAL.

**I HAVE** read, by order of the Lord Keeper of the Seals <note>The "Garde des Sceaux" was the head of the French judicial administration and the official responsible for the oversight of the book trade and censorship.</note>, a Manuscript entitled: *The Diadem of the Sages*, and I believe that its printing may be permitted. At Paris, this 3rd of February 1781.

->**LOURDET**, *Royal Professor.* <note>Jean-Pierre Lourdet was a doctor of theology and a Royal Censor who evaluated works on philosophy and religion.</note><-

# ROYAL PERMISSION.

**LOUIS**, BY THE GRACE OF GOD, King of France and Navarre: To our beloved and faithful Counselors, the People holding our Courts of Parliament, Masters of Requests in ordinary of our Household, Great Council, Provost of Paris, Bailiffs, Seneschals, their Civil Lieutenants, and our other Justices to whom it shall belong; GREETINGS. Our beloved Mr. *D. L.* <note>These initials likely refer to the author or the person seeking the publishing privilege.</note>, has set forth to Us that he desires to have printed and given to the Public a Work of his composition, entitled: *The Diadem of the Sages*, if it would please us to grant him our Letters of Permission necessary for this purpose. FOR THESE REASONS, wishing to treat the Petitioner favorably, we have permitted and do permit him by these Presents, to have the said Work printed as many times as he sees fit... <note>The text cuts off here, as this is a standard legal formula granting the author or publisher the exclusive right (a "privilège") to print the book for a set number of years.</note>

<meta>This page concludes the "Royal Permission" (Privilège du Roi), which grants exclusive printing rights. It specifies the final recipients of official copies (the Chancellor and the Keeper of the Seals), issues the formal command to enforce these rights regardless of local regional laws, and records the document's entry into the official guild register in Paris.</meta>

in that <note>Referring back to the mandatory deposit of manuscript copies mentioned on the previous page.</note> of our very dear and faithful Knight,
<term>Chancellor of France</term><note>The highest judicial officer in pre-revolutionary France.</note>, the Lord of <term>MAUPEOU</term><note>René Nicolas de Maupeou (1714–1792), a prominent French politician and Chancellor.</note>,
and one in that of the said Lord <term>HUE DE MIROMESNIL</term><note>Armand Thomas Hue de Miromesnil (1723–1796), who served as the Keeper of the Seals.</note>;
all under penalty of nullity of these <term>Presents</term><note>This legal document.</note>:
by the contents of which we command and en-
join you to let the said Petitioner and his <term>successors</term>
enjoy, fully and peacefully, without suf-
fering any trouble or hin-
drance to be caused to them. WE WILL that to the copy of these Presents,
which shall be printed in full, at the begin-
ning or at the end of the said Work, legal faith be
given as if to the original. We command the
first of our <term>Bailiffs</term> or <term>Sergeants</term> so re-
quested, to perform for the execution of these, all
required and necessary Acts, without asking other
permission, and notwithstanding <term>Clameur de Haro</term><note>original: "clameur de Haro". An ancient Norman legal custom where a person could stop a perceived injustice by shouting "Haro!"; here, the King is overriding this local right.</note>,
the <term>Norman Charter</term><note>original: "Charte Normande". A 1315 document granting specific privileges and autonomy to Normandy; the King is asserting that his decree takes precedence over these local provincial rights.</note>, and Letters to the contrary.
FOR such is our pleasure. GIVEN at Paris, the
second day of the month of May, the year of grace
one thousand seven hundred and eighty-one, and of our Reign
the seventh <note>Louis XVI began his reign in 1774; 1781 marks his seventh year on the throne.</note>. By the King in his Council.

->Signed, <term>LE BEGUE</term>.<-

*Registered on Register XXI of the <term>Royal &
Syndical Chamber of Booksellers & Printers

with the provisions set forth in the present Per-

<summary>This page concludes the legal authorization for printing the book, signed in 1781 under Louis XVI. It grants the publisher exclusive rights, overrides local Norman laws to ensure national enforcement, and notes the formal registration with the Parisian book guild.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the formal legal requirements for the book's publication. It finishes the record of the registration with the Booksellers' Guild (begun on the previous page) and provides the final colophon, detailing the exact date printing was finished and the identity of the printer.</meta>

...ber <note>This fragment completes the date of the registration entry into the official guild ledger mentioned on the previous page.</note>, *the eight copies prescribed by Article CVIII of the <term>Regulation of 1723</term><note>Known as the "Code de la Librairie," this law consolidated rules for the French book trade, including the "legal deposit" system where copies had to be provided to official libraries and censors.</note>. At Paris, this 18th of May, 1781.*

->LE CLERC, <term>Syndic</term><note>The chief official or warden of the Paris Guild of Printers and Booksellers, responsible for ensuring all books met legal and quality standards.</note>.<-

---

Printing completed, for the first time, on October 20, 1781.

---

->From the Printing House of QUILLAU, Printer to <term>His Serene Highness</term><note>original: "S. A. S. Mgr." (Son Altesse Sérénissime Monseigneur).</note> the <term>Prince of CONTI</term><note>Louis François Joseph de Bourbon (1734–1814), a prince of the blood. Having a royal or princely patron was a mark of high status for a printing business.</note>, rue du <term>Fouarre</term><note>A historic street in Paris's Latin Quarter, famous since the Middle Ages as a hub for scholars and the book trade.</note>.<-

<summary>This colophon marks the official completion of the book's production, confirming the legal deposit of copies and identifying Quillau as the authorized printer under the patronage of the Prince of Conti.</summary>
